People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me today, I really appreciate you taking the time. 

Um... I guess what made me come in is that... well, I hope I'm not making a big deal out of nothing, but the things that usually make me feel like myself have just started feeling sort of... flat? And heavy. Like, I usually love going on my photography walks, or mentoring the kids, or even just keeping a really clean calendar for work. I usually take a lot of pride in being organized and helpful. But lately, I just... I stopped initiating those things. 

I feel a little embarrassed even admitting that, honestly, because it sounds lazy, and I always try to be on top of everything. But I just haven't been able to get myself to do them. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it okay.

Um... no, not really before this. It's mostly just been this current stretch, honestly. It’s been going on for about a year and three months now, I think. 

It started right after a family member of mine... well, their health and behavior started changing, and I stepped in to take on a lot more of the coordination for them. Um... no, not really. I don't think so. I mean, everyone has bad days, of course, or times when they feel a little down, but... no, I've always been a pretty positive person. 

I really pride myself on being the one people can rely on, you know? At work, as an executive assistant, I have to be the one holding it all together, keeping a good attitude, making sure everything runs smoothly. So I've always just sort of... kept moving forward. I've never really let myself get stuck in a rut like this before. 

I hope that's okay? I know sometimes it helps to have a history to look back on, but this really is the first time I've felt this... heavy... for this long. Does that make sense?

Um... well, my appetite has been pretty normal, I think. I try to make sure I eat so I have energy for everything I need to do. 

But my sleep... um, it's been a little different lately. Not bad, necessarily! Just... different. I love them, and I'm glad I can be there for them. It's just... I think taking all of that on is maybe what tipped the scales for me. Is it okay to admit that? That helping them might be what triggered this?\n\nDoctor: When you're having these heavy, flat feelings — are they pretty much there all the time throughout your day, or do they come and go depending on what you're doing or how your family member is doing that day?\n\nPatient: Um... I'd say they're pretty much there all the time. It doesn't really seem to matter if it's a good day with my family member or a tough one, or even if I'm at work or at home. \n\nLike I mentioned before—and I hope I'm not repeating myself too much—but that's actually the main reason I finally made the appointment. The things I used to love, like my photography or mentoring, or even just sitting down to organize my week... they just feel so flat now. I try to push through, but there's just this constant heaviness to everything, and I realized I'm just not initiating any of those activities anymore. \n\nI really try to keep a smile on and not let it show, especially at work, but it's just sort of... always sitting there in the background. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it right.\n\nDoctor: Besides the heaviness and loss of interest in things you enjoy, have you noticed any changes in your sleep — like sleeping more or less than usual — or changes in your appetite over these past 15 months?\n\nPatient: Um... well, my appetite has been pretty normal, I think. I try to make sure I eat so I have energy for everything I need to do. \n\nBut my sleep... um, it's been a little different lately. Not bad, necessarily! Just... different. There have been nights where I only sleep maybe two or three hours, and then I wake up way before dawn. But the thing is, I don't feel tired. I actually feel... really productive? I'll get up and feel completely ready to start answering emails or updating my appointment lists for work. \n\nI guess it's actually been kind of helpful, getting a head start on the day like that, so I haven't really complained about it. I hope that's not a bad thing? I know people say you need eight hours, but... if I feel okay and I'm getting things done, that's alright, isn't it?\n\n[Last Question]\nYou mentioned waking up after only two or three hours of sleep but feeling productive and ready to go — have there been other times in your life, even before this 15-month period, when you had stretches where you needed much less sleep than usual but still felt energized and didn't feel tired?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / executive_secretary_or_executive_administrative_assistant / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Decreased_Sleep\nmanifestation: \"There have been nights when I sleep two or three hours, wake up before dawn, and feel ready to answer emails or update appointment lists instead of feeling tired.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Um... gosh, let me think. Have there been other times? Um... 

Well, like I said, recently there have been those nights where I only sleep two or three hours and wake up way before dawn, and I just feel totally ready to tackle my emails and update my appointment lists... instead of feeling tired, you know? It's been really nice to get that head start. 

But as for before this whole 15-month period... um, I'm trying to remember. I don't want to give you the wrong answer. I usually take a lot of pride in being organized and helpful. But lately, I just... I stopped initiating those things. \n\nI feel a little embarrassed even admitting that, honestly, because it sounds lazy, and I always try to be on top of everything. But I just haven't been able to get myself to do them. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it okay.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods like this before in your life — times when you felt this same heaviness and loss of interest in things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really before this. It's mostly just been this current stretch, honestly. It’s been going on for about a year and three months now, I think. \n\nIt started right after a family member of mine... well, their health and behavior started changing, and I stepped in to take on a lot more of the coordination for them. Which I am completely fine with doing! I always want to be supportive, and since I do admin work for a living, organizing things comes naturally to me. But I guess maybe taking on all those extra logistics just wore me down a little bit more than I wanted to admit? \n\nI hope it doesn't sound selfish or like I'm complaining about helping my family, because I really do want to be there for them. I just... I want to make sure I'm giving you the right context. Is it normal to get this bogged down when you take on extra responsibilities like that?\n\nDoctor: When you think back to when this heaviness and loss of interest started about a year and three months ago — did it come on pretty suddenly right after you took on those extra responsibilities for your family member, or did it gradually build up over weeks or months as you were managing everything?\n\nPatient: Um... I guess I'd say it was more gradual? It definitely didn't happen overnight. \n\nAt first, I was just doing what needed to be done, you know? Making the calls, setting up the appointments, keeping the schedules straight. I was happy to do it. But then, over... I don't know, maybe a few months... I just started feeling a little more tired at the end of the day. And then I noticed I wasn't grabbing my camera on the weekends anymore. \n\nI really hope I'm not making it sound like my family member is a burden, because they absolutely aren't. I just think maybe I wasn't pacing myself as well as I should have been? I always want to be the person who can handle everything with a smile, so I probably just pushed myself a little too hard without realizing it until the heaviness sort of... set in. Does that sound like a reasonable way for something like this to happen?\n\nDoctor: When you think back to those first few months after you started helping your family member, were there any particular weeks or days where you noticed a shift — like a moment when things started feeling noticeably heavier — or was it so gradual that you can't really pinpoint when it began?\n\nPatient: Um... I don't think I can really point to a specific day or even a specific week, to be honest. It was just so slow. \n\nI think I just kept telling myself, \"Oh, it's just a long week, next week will be better,\" and I'd just keep pushing through. I really try to be resilient, you know? I don't like to complain when things get a little busy. So I think I just ignored it for a long time until I looked back and realized, \"Oh, I haven't been on a photo walk in a really long time.\" \n\nI'm sorry, I know that's probably not very helpful for a timeline. I hope it's okay that I can't give you a more exact date? I just really didn't notice it happening until it was already there.\n\nDoctor: Over these past 15 months, has the heaviness and loss of interest in your hobbies gotten worse, stayed about the same, or actually improved at all — even if just a little bit — since you first started noticing it?\n\nPatient: Um... Like there was this weight attached to them. And I realized I had just... stopped initiating any of it. \n\nI hope it doesn't sound like I'm just giving up or being lazy. At work, as an executive assistant, I have to be the one holding it all together, keeping a good attitude, making sure everything runs smoothly. So I've always just sort of... kept moving forward. I usually take a lot of pride in being organized and helpful. But lately, I just... I stopped initiating those things. \n\nI feel a little embarrassed even admitting that, honestly, because it sounds lazy, and I always try to be on top of everything. But I just haven't been able to get myself to do them. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it okay.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods like this before in your life — times when you felt this same heaviness and loss of interest in things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really before this. It's mostly just been this current stretch, honestly. It’s been going on for about a year and three months now, I think. \n\nIt started right after a family member of mine... well, their health and behavior started changing, and I stepped in to take on a lot more of the coordination for them. Which I am completely fine with doing! I always want to be supportive, and since I do admin work for a living, organizing things comes naturally to me. But I guess maybe taking on all those extra logistics just wore me down a little bit more than I wanted to admit? \n\nI hope it doesn't sound selfish or like I'm complaining about helping my family, because I really do want to be there for them. I just... I want to make sure I'm giving you the right context. Is it normal to get this bogged down when you take on extra responsibilities like that?\n\nDoctor: When you think back to when this heaviness and loss of interest started about a year and three months ago — did it come on pretty suddenly right after you took on those extra responsibilities for your family member, or did it gradually build up over weeks or months as you were managing everything?\n\nPatient: Um... I guess I'd say it was more gradual? It definitely didn't happen overnight. \n\nAt first, I was just doing what needed to be done, you know? Making the calls, setting up the appointments, keeping the schedules straight. I was happy to do it. But then, over... I don't know, maybe a few months... I just started feeling a little more tired at the end of the day. And then I noticed I wasn't grabbing my camera on the weekends anymore. \n\nI really hope I'm not making it sound like my family member is a burden, because they absolutely aren't. I just think maybe I wasn't pacing myself as well as I should have been? I always want to be the person who can handle everything with a smile, so I probably just pushed myself a little too hard without realizing it until the heaviness sort of... set in. Does that sound like a reasonable way for something like this to happen?\n\nDoctor: When you think back to those first few months after you started helping your family member, were there any particular weeks or days where you noticed a shift — like a moment when things started feeling noticeably heavier — or was it so gradual that you can't really pinpoint when it began?\n\nPatient: Um... I don't think I can really point to a specific day or even a specific week, to be honest. It was just so slow. \n\nI think I just kept telling myself, \"Oh, it's just a long week, next week will be better,\" and I'd just keep pushing through. I really try to be resilient, you know? I don't like to complain when things get a little busy. So I think I just ignored it for a long time until I looked back and realized, \"Oh, I haven't been on a photo walk in a really long time.\" \n\nI'm sorry, I know that's probably not very helpful for a timeline. I hope it's okay that I can't give you a more exact date? I just really didn't notice it happening until it was already there.\n\nDoctor: Over these past 15 months, has the heaviness and loss of interest in your hobbies gotten worse, stayed about the same, or actually improved at all — even if just a little bit — since you first started noticing it?\n\nPatient: Um... I'd say it's gotten a little worse, which is... well, that's actually what finally made me come in to see you. \n\nI really tried to just power through it, because I know everyone gets tired, right? But the things that usually make me feel like myself... like going on my photography walks, or mentoring the kids in my neighborhood, or honestly, even just keeping my own calendar perfectly organized... they all just started feeling so flat. Like there was this weight attached to them. And I realized I had just... stopped initiating any of it. \n\nI hope it doesn't sound like I'm just giving up or being lazy. I really do want to be doing those things, I just... I couldn't seem to find the spark for them anymore. Is it normal to lose your drive for the things you love like that when you get overwhelmed?\n\nDoctor: I want to make sure I have the full picture. Looking back even further — before this 15-month period started — have there been any other times in your life, even brief ones, when you felt depressed, sad, or lost interest in things for weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. I don't think so. I mean, everyone has bad days, of course, or times when they feel a little down, but... no, I've always been a pretty positive person. \n\nI really pride myself on being the one people can rely on, you know? At work, as an executive assistant, I have to be the one holding it all together, keeping a good attitude, making sure everything runs smoothly. So I've always just sort of... kept moving forward. I've never really let myself get stuck in a rut like this before. \n\nI hope that's okay? I know sometimes it helps to have a history to look back on, but this really is the first time I've felt this... heavy... for this long. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you think about the timing — your family member's health and behavior started changing, and then you took on those extra responsibilities for them — did your heaviness and loss of interest start around that same time, or did it begin noticeably before their situation came up?\n\nPatient: Um... it definitely started after. Yeah, it was after. \n\nI'd say it's been about a year and three months now, total, since I really started feeling this way. It definitely wasn't before they needed me. When their health and, um, their behavior started changing, and I had to step in and take on all that extra coordination for them... that's when I started to feel it. \n\nI was just trying to be helpful, you know? I wanted to make sure they were taken care of, because that's what family does. I really hope it doesn't sound like I'm blaming them, or saying they're a burden, because I don't feel that way at all. I love them, and I'm glad I can be there for them. It's just... I think taking all of that on is maybe what tipped the scales for me. Is it okay to admit that? That helping them might be what triggered this?\n\nDoctor: When you're having these heavy, flat feelings — are they pretty much there all the time throughout your day, or do they come and go depending on what you're doing or how your family member is doing that day?\n\nPatient: Um... I'd say they're pretty much there all the time. It doesn't really seem to matter if it's a good day with my family member or a tough one, or even if I'm at work or at home. \n\nLike I mentioned before—and I hope I'm not repeating myself too much—but that's actually the main reason I finally made the appointment. The things I used to love, like my photography or mentoring, or even just sitting down to organize my week... they just feel so flat now. I try to push through, but there's just this constant heaviness to everything, and I realized I'm just not initiating any of those activities anymore. \n\nI really try to keep a smile on and not let it show, especially at work, but it's just sort of... always sitting there in the background. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it right.\n\nDoctor: Besides the heaviness and loss of interest in things you enjoy, have you noticed any changes in your sleep — like sleeping more or less than usual — or changes in your appetite over these past 15 months?\n\nPatient: Um... well, my appetite has been pretty normal, I think. I try to make sure I eat so I have energy for everything I need to do. \n\nBut my sleep... um, it's been a little different lately. Not bad, necessarily! Just... different. There have been nights where I only sleep maybe two or three hours, and then I wake up way before dawn. But the thing is, I don't feel tired. I actually feel... really productive? I'll get up and feel completely ready to start answering emails or updating my appointment lists for work. \n\nI guess it's actually been kind of helpful, getting a head start on the day like that, so I haven't really complained about it. I hope that's not a bad thing? I know people say you need eight hours, but... if I feel okay and I'm getting things done, that's alright, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: You mentioned waking up after only two or three hours of sleep but feeling productive and ready to go — have there been other times in your life, even before this 15-month period, when you had stretches where you needed much less sleep than usual but still felt energized and didn't feel tired?\n\nPatient: Um... gosh, let me think. Have there been other times? Um... \n\nWell, like I said, recently there have been those nights where I only sleep two or three hours and wake up way before dawn, and I just feel totally ready to tackle my emails and update my appointment lists... instead of feeling tired, you know? It's been really nice to get that head start. \n\nBut as for before this whole 15-month period... um, I'm trying to remember. I don't want to give you the wrong answer. I suppose... maybe there have been a few stretches in the past where I didn't need as much sleep and got a lot done? But I always just thought of it as being a really dedicated worker. You know, going the extra mile? I hope that doesn't make me sound like I've been doing something wrong all these years. It's just... sometimes you get in a groove and you just want to keep being productive. Is that what you mean? I hope I'm answering your question right.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to those times in the past when you didn't need much sleep but felt productive and energized — during those stretches, did you also notice your mood was elevated or unusually good, or did you feel like you could accomplish more than you normally do?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah, I guess so. I mean, during those times, I definitely feel... unusually upbeat, I suppose? Like everything is just clicking perfectly and I can handle anything that comes my way. It feels really good, actually. I hope it's okay to admit that. \n\nBut, um... well, there's another side to it, I guess. I try really hard to always be professional and polite, especially as an executive assistant, but... sometimes during those stretches, if something tiny happens—like, if someone moves a meeting at the last minute or just interrupts my flow—I can get... well, a little sharp with people. \n\nIt actually surprises me when it happens, because that's really not who I try to be. I always want to be helpful and accommodating. I really hope that doesn't make me sound difficult to work with. It's just that when I'm in that zone, feeling so good and getting everything done, any little disruption just feels so frustrating. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not making myself sound awful.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to those times in the past when you felt really upbeat, needed less sleep, and got a lot accomplished — how long did those stretches typically last? Were they days, weeks, or longer?\n\nPatient: Um... let's see. How long did they last... um... \n\nWell, I've been noticing these sort of... really productive, energetic stretches where I don't need much sleep happening off and on for about eleven months now. They sort of come and go, I guess? \n\nI hope that doesn't sound too strange, having it happen for almost a year. I mean, I always tried to use those times to get ahead at work, so I thought I was just being a really dedicated employee, you know? I didn't want anyone to think I was slacking off or acting unprofessionally. I really pride myself on being reliable. \n\nIs eleven months... is that a normal amount of time for something like this to keep popping up? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information. I hope I haven't let it go on too long without saying something.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

  "candidates": ["F31.9"],
  "note": "Patient reports past episodes of decreased need for sleep with elevated mood, increased productivity, and irritability lasting weeks to months ('off and on for about eleven months now'). Current presentation includes depressed mood, anhedonia, and persistent heaviness for 15 months. This pattern of distinct mood episodes (hypomanic/manic periods alternating with depressive period) is consistent with bipolar disorder. F32.9 (Major Depressive Disorder) ruled out due to clear history of hypomanic episodes with decreased sleep need, elevated mood, and increased goal-directed activity.",
  "is_final": true
}
